>[!note] Autoencoders
>An **autoencoder** is a network trained to output its own input. An **encoder** $f$ maps an input $\mathbf x \in \mathbb R^d$ to a **code** $\mathbf a = f(\mathbf x) \in \mathbb R^k$. A **decoder** $g$ maps the code back to a reconstruction $\hat{\mathbf x} = g(\mathbf a) \in \mathbb R^d$. Training on $n$ examples minimizes the reconstruction loss
>$$\mathcal L = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n} \big\|\mathbf x_i - g(f(\mathbf x_i))\big\|^2 .$$
>The label of each example is the example itself, so no annotation is needed. This makes the autoencoder a form of [[8.1. Self-Supervised Learning|self-supervised learning]]. Squared error suits real-valued data, and other data types use the loss that fits them. When $k < d$, the code is a **bottleneck**. The network must then compress each input into $k$ numbers.
>
>Stack the centered data points as the rows of $\mathbf X \in \mathbb R^{n \times d}$ and take the [[13.1 Singular Value Decomposition|SVD]] $\mathbf X = \mathbf U \boldsymbol\Sigma \mathbf V^T$. The singular values are $\sigma_1 \ge \sigma_2 \ge \dots \ge 0$, with left singular vectors $\mathbf u_i \in \mathbb R^n$ and right singular vectors $\mathbf v_i \in \mathbb R^d$. [[7.1. Principal Component Analysis|PCA]] encodes $\mathbf x$ by projecting it onto the top $k$ right singular vectors, $\mathbf a = \mathbf V_k^T \mathbf x$, where $\mathbf V_k = [\mathbf v_1, \dots, \mathbf v_k] \in \mathbb R^{d \times k}$. It decodes with $\hat{\mathbf x} = \mathbf V_k \mathbf a$. The [[13.3 Low-Rank Approximation|Eckart-Young theorem]] says this is the best rank-$k$ reconstruction:
>$$\hat{\mathbf X}_k = \sum_{i=1}^{k} \sigma_i \mathbf u_i \mathbf v_i^T = \mathbf X \mathbf V_k \mathbf V_k^T = \arg\min_{\operatorname{rank}(\mathbf Y) \le k} \|\mathbf X - \mathbf Y\|_F^2, \qquad \|\mathbf X - \hat{\mathbf X}_k\|_F^2 = \sum_{i > k} \sigma_i^2 .$$
>A **linear autoencoder** uses $\mathbf a = \mathbf W_1 \mathbf x$ and $\hat{\mathbf x} = \mathbf W_2 \mathbf a$, with $\mathbf W_1 \in \mathbb R^{k \times d}$, $\mathbf W_2 \in \mathbb R^{d \times k}$ and no biases. Its total squared error over the data is $\|\mathbf X - \mathbf X \mathbf W_1^T \mathbf W_2^T\|_F^2 = n\mathcal L$. If $\sigma_k > \sigma_{k+1}$, every global minimum reconstructs the data exactly as PCA does, with total squared error $\sum_{i>k}\sigma_i^2$. If $\mathbf X$ also has rank $d$, every global minimum has the form
>$$\mathbf W_1 = \mathbf M \mathbf V_k^T, \qquad \mathbf W_2 = \mathbf V_k \mathbf M^{-1}, \qquad \mathbf W_2 \mathbf W_1 = \mathbf V_k \mathbf V_k^T$$
>for some invertible $\mathbf M \in \mathbb R^{k \times k}$. The rows of $\mathbf W_1$ therefore span the top-$k$ principal subspace. They are the principal axes themselves only when $\mathbf M$ is a permutation matrix times a diagonal matrix.
>
>A **nonlinear autoencoder** builds $f$ and $g$ from layers with nonlinear [[3. Activation Functions|activations]], such as MLPs or CNNs. It can represent data that lie near a curved surface, which no flat subspace fits. A $100 \times 100$ image has $10{,}000$ pixel values, and an encoder might compress it to a code of $128$ numbers. Three variants change what the network sees or what the code may do:
>
>- **Denoising autoencoder**: the input is a corrupted copy $\tilde{\mathbf x}$, such as $\mathbf x$ plus noise, and the target is the clean $\mathbf x$. The corruption is generated during training, as in [[4.4.2. Data Augmentation|data augmentation]].
>- **Masked autoencoder**: random parts of the input, such as image patches, are hidden. The network must fill in the hidden parts from the visible ones. The loss compares only the hidden parts with the originals.
>- **Sparse autoencoder**: the code may be as wide as the input or wider, $k \ge d$. A penalty keeps most code entries at zero for each input.
>
>All three stop the network from learning the identity map, which would copy the input and learn nothing.

>[!warning] Rows Versus Columns
>Both this note and [[7.1. Principal Component Analysis]] store data points as the rows of $\mathbf X$. The principal directions are then the right singular vectors $\mathbf v_i$ of $\mathbf X$. Some texts store data points as the columns of $A = \mathbf X^T \in \mathbb R^{d \times n}$. There the same directions are the left singular vectors of $A$. Both versions subtract the mean data point first. Without centering, the first direction tends to point toward the mean.

>[!warning] Not Taught in Lecture
>The form of the sparsity penalty was not given. A common choice adds $\lambda \|\mathbf a\|_1$ to the loss, with a weight $\lambda > 0$. This form is from Goodfellow, Bengio and Courville, *Deep Learning* (2016), section 14.2.1.

# Intuition

Labels are scarce, while inputs are plentiful. An autoencoder turns every input into its own training target. Copying an input is easy, so the network needs a constraint. A bottleneck is one such constraint. Suppose a network must pass $10{,}000$ pixel values through $128$ numbers. It then has to find features that the images share, such as edges, shapes and lighting. The code records each image's values of those shared features. The figure shows the shape. The encoder narrows, the code is the narrowest point, and the decoder widens back to the input size.


PCA solves the linear version of this problem in closed form. It keeps the $k$ directions along which the data vary most and drops the rest. Gradient descent on a linear autoencoder finds the same $k$-dimensional subspace, but it may describe that subspace in any basis. A nonlinear encoder can also map data that lie on a curved surface to flat coordinates. No linear projection can do that.

The next animation shows a linear autoencoder with a two-number code on $20$ points in 3D. Training turns its plane toward the PCA plane, and the error falls to $\sigma_3^2$. At the end, every residual points along the dropped direction $\mathbf v_3$.


The last animation compares PCA with a nonlinear autoencoder on the five parabola points from the Hard example. Both use one number per point. PCA gives two pairs of points the same code. The nonlinear encoder flattens the curve, and the decoder bends it back.

# Properties

#### *(Only the Subspace Is Learned)* The loss of a linear autoencoder depends only on the product $\mathbf W_2 \mathbf W_1$, so training cannot single out the principal axes.

Replacing $\mathbf W_1$ by $\mathbf M\mathbf W_1$ and $\mathbf W_2$ by $\mathbf W_2\mathbf M^{-1}$ leaves the product and every reconstruction unchanged. Starting from PCA, the code becomes $\mathbf M\mathbf a$, which mixes and rescales the PCA coordinates. Its entries are correlated in general, while PCA coordinates are uncorrelated. The same freedom appears in a [[3.12.1 Kalman Filter|Kalman filter]] learned from controls and observations alone. Its state is then known only up to a change of basis. Tying the weights, $\mathbf W_2 = \mathbf W_1^T$, restricts $\mathbf M$ to orthogonal matrices, so a rotation remains. To recover the axes, orthonormalize the rows of $\mathbf W_1$ and project the data onto them. Run PCA on these $k$-dimensional projections, which is cheap. Mapping its directions back through the orthonormal rows gives $\mathbf v_1, \dots, \mathbf v_k$.

The animation shows this freedom on the points from the 3D animation above. The code grid on the PCA plane turns, stretches and shears with $\mathbf M$. The reconstructions and the loss stay the same.

#### *(Reading the Error)* The best error of a rank-$k$ code is the sum of the dropped squared singular values.

We can use this number to choose $k$. A rank-$k$ code keeps the fraction $\sum_{i\le k}\sigma_i^2 / \sum_i \sigma_i^2$ of the data's squared norm. With centered data, this is the fraction of variance explained. A sharp drop after $\sigma_k$ means the data lie close to a $k$-dimensional subspace, and a larger $k$ adds little. A slow decay means no small linear code fits well. A nonlinear autoencoder may still compress such data if they lie near a curved surface. The PCA error is therefore the baseline. A nonlinear autoencoder with code size $k$ is useful only if it beats PCA with the same $k$ on held-out data.

#### *(Denoising Shrinks Weak Directions)* A linear denoising autoencoder without a bottleneck keeps strong directions of the data and damps weak ones.

>[!warning] Not Taught in Lecture
>The denoising autoencoder was introduced without analysis. The link between input noise and a ridge penalty is from Bishop (1995). The paper is *Training with Noise Is Equivalent to Tikhonov Regularization*.

Add Gaussian noise $\mathbf n \sim \mathcal N(\mathbf 0, \tau^2 \mathbf I)$ to the input of a linear map $\mathbf W \in \mathbb R^{d\times d}$. The expected loss for one example is
$$\mathbb E\,\big\|\mathbf x - \mathbf W(\mathbf x + \mathbf n)\big\|^2 = \|\mathbf x - \mathbf W\mathbf x\|^2 + \tau^2 \|\mathbf W\|_F^2 .$$
The noise acts as a ridge penalty on $\mathbf W$. Let $\mathbf S = \frac1n \mathbf X^T\mathbf X$ be the second-moment matrix of the data. Averaged over the data, the minimizer is $\mathbf W = \mathbf S(\mathbf S + \tau^2\mathbf I)^{-1}$. Along the principal direction $\mathbf v_i$ it multiplies by $\lambda_i/(\lambda_i + \tau^2)$, where $\lambda_i = \sigma_i^2/n$. Directions with much more variance than the noise pass almost unchanged, and directions with less are damped toward zero. The identity map is never optimal when $\tau > 0$, so the network learns about the data even with $k = d$. PCA keeps each direction fully or drops it. This map scales each direction by a factor between $0$ and $1$. On the quiz data of the first example below, $\tau^2 = 0.5$ gives the factors $0.9$ and $0.5$.

The animation raises the noise on the quiz data from the first example below. The chart on the right tracks the factor $\lambda_i/(\lambda_i + \tau^2)$ for each principal direction.


#### *(Memorization Through a Small Code)* A powerful enough encoder and decoder can memorize the training set through a one-number code.

>[!warning] Not Taught in Lecture
>This failure mode is from Goodfellow, Bengio and Courville (2016), section 14.1.

The encoder could map the $i$-th training example to the number $i$. The decoder could then map $i$ back to $\mathbf x_i$. The training loss would be zero, and the code would describe nothing about the data. This extreme is a thought experiment, but the risk grows with the size of the encoder and decoder. A small code alone therefore does not guarantee useful features. A memorized lookup fails on new inputs, so its reconstruction error on held-out data is large.

#### *(Encoder as Feature Extractor)* After training, the decoder is usually discarded and the code feeds another task.

A classifier trained on the codes can need far fewer labeled examples than one trained on raw pixels. This holds only if the code kept the information the classifier needs. Pretraining an encoder this way is the main use of autoencoders in self-supervised learning. The decoder is there only to train the encoder. Under the reconstruction loss, the code keeps whatever contributes most to the squared error. That need not match what the later task needs. An image code may spend capacity on background brightness that a classifier ignores.

# Derivation

>[!warning] Not Taught in Lecture
>The lecture stated the Eckart-Young theorem next to the linear autoencoder but did not prove that their optima agree. The result is from Baldi and Hornik (1989), *Neural Networks and Principal Component Analysis: Learning from Examples Without Local Minima*. They also studied the other critical points. Suppose the eigenvalues of $\mathbf X^T\mathbf X$ are distinct and nonzero. Then every critical point other than the global minima is a saddle point.

We want the global minima of the total squared error $E(\mathbf W_1, \mathbf W_2) = \|\mathbf X - \mathbf X\mathbf W_1^T\mathbf W_2^T\|_F^2 = n\mathcal L$, starting from the [[13.3 Low-Rank Approximation|Eckart-Young theorem]]. The product $\mathbf P = \mathbf W_2 \mathbf W_1 \in \mathbb R^{d\times d}$ has rank at most $k$, because every input passes through $k$ numbers. Hence $\mathbf X\mathbf P^T$ has rank at most $k$, and Eckart-Young gives the lower bound
$$E \ge \sum_{i>k}\sigma_i^2 .$$
The choice $\mathbf W_1 = \mathbf V_k^T$ and $\mathbf W_2 = \mathbf V_k$ gives $\mathbf X\mathbf V_k\mathbf V_k^T = \hat{\mathbf X}_k$, which meets the bound. The bound's value follows from $\mathbf X - \hat{\mathbf X}_k = \sum_{i>k}\sigma_i\mathbf u_i\mathbf v_i^T$. Its terms are orthogonal, so its squared Frobenius norm is $\sum_{i>k}\sigma_i^2$.

When $\sigma_k > \sigma_{k+1}$, $\hat{\mathbf X}_k$ is the unique best rank-$k$ approximation. Every global minimum therefore satisfies $\mathbf X\mathbf P^T = \mathbf X\mathbf V_k\mathbf V_k^T$. If $\mathbf X$ has rank $d$, multiplying on the left by $(\mathbf X^T\mathbf X)^{-1}\mathbf X^T$ gives $\mathbf P = \mathbf V_k\mathbf V_k^T$. This $\mathbf P$ has rank $k$, so $\mathbf W_2$ and $\mathbf W_1$ both have rank $k$. The columns of $\mathbf W_2$ then span the column space of $\mathbf P$, which is the span of $\mathbf v_1, \dots, \mathbf v_k$. So $\mathbf W_2 = \mathbf V_k\mathbf N$ for an invertible $\mathbf N \in \mathbb R^{k\times k}$. The same argument on rows gives $\mathbf W_1 = \mathbf M\mathbf V_k^T$. Substituting,
$$\mathbf V_k \mathbf N\mathbf M\mathbf V_k^T = \mathbf V_k\mathbf V_k^T \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \mathbf N\mathbf M = \mathbf I_k ,$$
after multiplying by $\mathbf V_k^T$ on the left and $\mathbf V_k$ on the right. Hence $\mathbf N = \mathbf M^{-1}$, which is the form stated in the callout.

# Examples

>[!example] (Easy) Compressing Two Quiz Scores Into One Number
>Four students take two quizzes. After subtracting the class average from each quiz, their scores form the rows of
>$$\mathbf X = \begin{bmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ -2 & -1 \\ 1 & 2 \\ -1 & -2 \end{bmatrix}.$$
>
>>[!example]- Find the rank-1 PCA reconstruction, its error, and the share of variance it keeps.
>>The matrix $\mathbf X^T\mathbf X = \begin{bmatrix} 10 & 8 \\ 8 & 10\end{bmatrix}$ has eigenvalues $18$ and $2$, with eigenvectors $\mathbf v_1 = \tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}(1,1)$ and $\mathbf v_2 = \tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}(1,-1)$. The singular values are $\sigma_1 = \sqrt{18} \approx 4.243$ and $\sigma_2 = \sqrt2 \approx 1.414$. Each student's code is $a = \mathbf v_1^T\mathbf x = (x_1 + x_2)/\sqrt2$. This is $3/\sqrt2 \approx 2.121$ for students 1 and 3 and $-2.121$ for students 2 and 4. The reconstruction $\hat{\mathbf x} = a\,\mathbf v_1$ replaces both scores with their average:
>>$$\hat{\mathbf X}_1 = \begin{bmatrix} 1.5 & 1.5 \\ -1.5 & -1.5 \\ 1.5 & 1.5 \\ -1.5 & -1.5 \end{bmatrix}.$$
>>Every row misses by $\pm(0.5, -0.5)$, a squared error of $0.5$. The total is $2 = \sigma_2^2$, as Eckart-Young predicts. The code keeps $18/20 = 90\%$ of the variance. It records each student's overall level and drops the difference between the two quizzes. Students 1 and 3 get the same code, so the code cannot tell them apart.

>[!example] (Medium) Two Linear Autoencoders on the Same Data
>A linear autoencoder with $d = 2$ and $k = 1$ is trained on the quiz data above. One training run ends at $\mathbf W_1 = [2,\ 2]$ and $\mathbf W_2 = [0.25,\ 0.25]^T$. A second design fixes the encoder to $\mathbf W_1' = [1,\ 0]$, which reads only the first quiz. It then fits the best decoder for that encoder.
>
>>[!example]- Compute the first student's code and reconstruction under each design, and each total loss. Which design is a global minimum?
>>In the first design, the code of $\mathbf x = (2,1)$ is $a = 2\cdot2 + 2\cdot1 = 6$. The reconstruction is $(0.25\cdot6,\ 0.25\cdot6) = (1.5, 1.5)$, which matches PCA. The product is
>>$$\mathbf W_2\mathbf W_1 = \begin{bmatrix}0.5 & 0.5\\0.5&0.5\end{bmatrix} = \mathbf v_1\mathbf v_1^T,$$
>>so every reconstruction matches PCA and the total squared error is the minimum, $2$. The code is the PCA code $3/\sqrt2$ times $M = 2\sqrt2 \approx 2.828$. Here $\mathbf W_1 = M\mathbf v_1^T$ and $\mathbf W_2 = \mathbf v_1/M$, the form from the callout.
>>
>>In the second design, the codes are $\mathbf a = (2, -2, 1, -1)$. The best decoder is the least-squares fit $\mathbf W_2' = \mathbf X^T\mathbf a/(\mathbf a^T\mathbf a) = (10, 8)/10 = (1, 0.8)$. The first student's code is $2$, and the reconstruction is $(2, 1.6)$. The four reconstructions are $(2, 1.6)$, $(-2, -1.6)$, $(1, 0.8)$ and $(-1, -0.8)$. Their squared errors are $0.36$, $0.36$, $1.44$ and $1.44$, a total of $3.6$. This exceeds $2$ because the encoder's row $[1, 0]$ lies outside the principal subspace. Only the first design is a global minimum. The scale of $\mathbf W_1$ does not matter, but its direction does.

>[!example] (Hard) A Curve That PCA Cannot Compress
>Five sensor readings lie on a parabola, at the points $(t, t^2)$ for $t = -2, -1, 0, 1, 2$. After centering, the second coordinate becomes $t^2 - 2$.
>
>>[!example]- Compare the best rank-1 linear code with a nonlinear autoencoder that has a one-number code.
>>The centered rows are $(-2, 2)$, $(-1, -1)$, $(0, -2)$, $(1, -1)$ and $(2, 2)$. Then $\mathbf X^T\mathbf X = \operatorname{diag}(10, 14)$, so $\sigma_1^2 = 14$ along the second axis and $\sigma_2^2 = 10$ along the first. PCA keeps only the coordinate $t^2 - 2$. Its error is $10$, and it keeps $14/24 \approx 58\%$ of the variance. The points at $t = -1$ and $t = 1$ get the same code, so PCA cannot tell them apart.
>>
>>A nonlinear autoencoder can reach zero error. Let the encoder output $a = x_1 = t$. The decoder must output $(a, a^2 - 2)$ at $a = -2, -1, 0, 1, 2$. Its first output is $a$ itself. For the second, a ReLU layer with four units gives
>>$$g_2(a) = -2 + \mathrm{ReLU}(a) + \mathrm{ReLU}(-a) + 2\,\mathrm{ReLU}(a - 1) + 2\,\mathrm{ReLU}(-a - 1).$$
>>This equals $2, -1, -2, -1, 2$ at the five points. The code still has one number, and the nonlinear decoder maps it onto the curve. Between the data points $g_2$ is piecewise linear. For example, $g_2(0.5) = -1.5$, while the parabola gives $0.5^2 - 2 = -1.75$.
